We Misread one of the Most Influential Essays in AI History. Here's How.
We Misread one of the Most Influential Essays in AI History. Here's How.
Chip Joyce
Chip Joyce
Beyond AI Hype: C-Suite Strategy & Practical...
Fecha de publicación: 21 ago 2025
 Siguiendo
Most AI experts worship at the altar of "just add more compute." Here's why they might be dangerously wrong.
For years, Richard Sutton's "Bitter Lesson" has been Silicon Valley's north star: forget clever algorithms, just add more compute. The narrative is seductive—Deep Blue crushed Kasparov through brute force, not chess wisdom. AlphaGo demolished human Go masters with scale, not strategy. We didn't build ChatGPT by teaching it English like we teach children. We just fed it the entire internet and let raw computing power do the rest.

But here's what keeps me up at night: What if the algorithms that "lost" weren't actually the right ones?

The Untold Story
Consider this: When symbolic AI failed in the 1980s (the systems that tried to encode human expert knowledge directly), we lacked GPUs, distributed training, and internet-scale data. Those "clever" approaches weren't given a fair fight—they were bringing knowledge to a compute gunfight they couldn't win. It's like declaring horses superior to cars after testing a Model T on a muddy trail in 1910.

What if the algorithms that "lost" weren't actually the right ones?
Today's reality check:


Our "pure scaling" ChatGPT-style models need massive amounts of human feedback and careful data selection to work properly
Simple visual puzzles that 5-year-olds can solve still stump our best AI (researchers call this the ARC challenge—it tests whether AI truly understands patterns or just memorizes)
Energy costs and running out of quality training data loom like the hardware constraints of yesteryear
The Real Lesson May Be More Nuanced
What if the future isn't "scale beats algorithms" but "the right algorithms scale better"?

Think of it this way: A bicycle going 10x faster is still slower than a car going 2x faster. But more importantly, the car gets exponentially more benefit from a bigger engine than the bicycle gets from you pedaling harder. In AI terms: smart design doesn't just add to computing power—it multiplies it.

A bicycle going 10x faster is still slower than a car going 2x faster.
Three Uncomfortable Questions for 2025

Are we confusing correlation with causation? Just because making models bigger worked doesn't mean bigness is why they worked. Perhaps we've been accidentally discovering better designs through trial and error at massive scale.
What happens when the well runs dry? We're already feeding AI-generated content back to AIs to train them (creating echo chambers), and we're running low on new human-written content. Will we face our own "bitter lesson" about the bitter lesson?
Are we building intelligence or sophisticated mimicry? If real intelligence requires genuinely new ideas and understanding cause-and-effect (not just remixing training data), have we been climbing the wrong mountain entirely?
We're feeding AI slop to AI. What could go wrong?
The Path Forward
I'm not anti-scaling. But blind faith in any paradigm is dangerous. The next breakthrough might come from:


Hybrid systems that blend pattern recognition (what current AI does well) with logical reasoning (what humans do well)
AI that can genuinely create new solutions, not just recombine what it's seen before
Algorithms that achieve more with less, driven by necessity as resources plateau
The bitter lesson taught us that compute matters. The sweet lesson might be that compute + the right design principles = genuine intelligence.

What's your take? Are we due for a paradigm shift, or will scaling continue to surprise us?

This LinkedIn-style essay is engaging with a subtle but critical reinterpretation of Richard Sutton's
"The Bitter Lesson"
(2019). Sutton's original claim was that, historically, the most progress in AI has come not from carefully hand-engineered algorithms but from exploiting ever-increasing compute to run simple, general-purpose methods. Chip Joyce argues we may have collectively misread it by overemphasizing "just add more compute" while overlooking the possibility that
the "losing" algorithmic paradigms may simply have been starved of sufficient compute and data in their own era.
Here's a structured academic-style analysis:
1. The Received Interpretation of Sutton's Lesson
Scaling Orthodoxy
: The canonical reading is that general-purpose methods, especially when scaled with more compute, systematically outperform bespoke, domain-specific, "clever" algorithms.
Historical Evidence
: Chess (Deep Blue), Go (AlphaGo), and language models (GPT) seem to confirm that brute-force or deep learning scale is the winning path.
2. Joyce's Counterpoint: A Misreading
Unequal Playing Field
: Symbolic AI, expert systems, and other "knowledge-centric" paradigms of the 1970s-1990s collapsed under hardware limits, not necessarily intrinsic inferiority. Their failure may reflect premature evaluation.
Analogy
: Like testing a Model T against horses in 1910 and declaring cars permanently inferior.
3. Structural Constraints in Today's Scaling Paradigm
Diminishing Returns
: Scaling requires enormous human feedback and curated data. Models plateau without careful reinforcement (RLHF, RLAIF).
Energy & Data Bottlenecks
: Both high-quality text data and power budgets are running out, much as Moore's Law slowed.
Persistent Weaknesses
: Benchmarks like ARC (Abstraction and Reasoning Corpus) expose brittleness in pattern abstraction.
4. The Alternative Reading: Algorithmic Leverage
Multiplicative Design
: Good design multiplies compute's effectiveness (like a car vs. bicycle scaling analogy).
Hybrid Futures
: Symbolic + connectionist integration, causal reasoning, and sparse inference may become critical once raw scaling hits limits.
The "Sweet Lesson"
: Compute is necessary, but algorithmic architecture may determine whether scaling yields true intelligence.
5. Philosophical Questions Raised
Correlation vs. Causation
: Did scale work because of size, or because larger experiments accidentally surfaced better architectures?
Self-Consumption of Data
: Training AI on AI-generated slop risks a "degeneracy spiral."
Simulation vs. Intelligence
: Are we mistaking remixing for original cognition?
6. Relation to RSVP and Polycomputational Paradigms
From the standpoint of your own RSVP framework:
Recursive Causality vs. Brute Scaling
: RSVP models intelligence as entropic smoothing with structured recursion, not infinite brute force. This aligns with Joyce's claim that
right design multiplies scale
.
Hybrid Symbolic-Field Systems
: RSVP already gestures toward a post-scaling era: blending continuous fields (Φ, 𝒗, S) with semantic operators mirrors the "symbolic revival" Joyce hints at.
Polycomputation as Counter-Lesson
: Instead of "one scaling method beats all," RSVP suggests that multiple concurrent paradigms (symbolic, field-theoretic, stochastic) scale

through structured interaction.

1. What Sutton Originally Said
Richard Sutton's
Bitter Lesson
(2019) made a stark historical observation:
Whenever humans tried to design clever, domain-specific methods (like hand-crafted chess strategies, expert systems, or symbolic reasoning modules), they were eventually outperformed by simple, general-purpose methods that could exploit
more computation
and
more data
.
The "bitter" part: human insight and cleverness matter less than brute force scaling.
That reading has since become dogma:
don't waste time on fancy algorithms—just scale up simple ones.
2. The Reinterpretation
Chip Joyce is saying:
Wait—maybe we read Sutton too literally.
In the 1980s-1990s, symbolic AI, expert systems, and logic-based reasoning
did fail
—but
not because they were fundamentally wrong ideas
.
They failed because they were running on weak hardware, tiny datasets, and no internet connectivity. They brought "knowledge to a compute gunfight."
So the "losers" of history may not have been
bad algorithms
—they were just
premature
.
3. The Present Problem
Now we're seeing cracks in the brute-force scaling story:
Plateaus
: Large models still fail at simple reasoning tasks (e.g. ARC test puzzles).
Bottlenecks
: We're running out of high-quality human text to train on, and power costs are exploding.
Feedback loops
: Training AI on AI-generated output risks a degeneracy spiral.
This makes the blind "just scale it" philosophy look short-sighted.
4. The Alternative Lesson
So the refined claim is:
Scaling is powerful
—it
does

But

first
. Many older ideas were abandoned because they didn't scale back then.
Thus the real future might be:
"The right algorithms scale better" → once you combine scaling with more intelligent designs (symbolic + neural hybrids, causal models, sparse reasoning systems), you may get multiplicative benefits.

Think of it like this:
A bicycle rider going 10x faster still loses to a car that only improves 2x, because the car's design leverages improvements more effectively.
In AI terms: scaling amplifies everything, but

Yes

The core point

the enabler
. The "right algorithms" might be lying dormant, waiting for the compute that was missing in their time.

Technological modularity

—which interface or encoding happened to mesh best with the available compute, cost structures, or industrial incentives at the time.

Material constraints
: 2D reels were compact, cheap, and reproducible. 3D or local reshooting was costlier.
Industrial incentives
: Hollywood's economics favored
one master version

Feedback loop
: Once distribution and projection infrastructure standardized around 2D reels, alternative encodings became impractical.

The lesson: scaling
amplifies whatever encoding you start with
. If you start with frames, you get cinema as we know it; if you start with 3D scene graphs, you might get something closer to VR by 1930. If you start with tokens, you get LLMs; if you start with RSVP-style fields, you might get entirely different intelligences.

Excellent. Here is a first draft of the comparative essay you suggested. I've written it in academic prose, with clear sections, tying your cinema counterfactuals to the AI "Bitter Lesson" debate and RSVP.
Roads Not Taken: Cinema, Computation, and the Bitter Lesson
Introduction
The history of technology often appears linear in hindsight: film evolved from still photography to moving pictures to sound; computing evolved from punched cards to keyboards to digital networks. Yet such linearity conceals the
contingency of encoding standards
—the arbitrary-seeming representational choices that, once standardized, shape the entire trajectory of a medium. This essay explores how alternative encodings in cinema (volumetric scenes, local actor substitution) and computation (cursive input/output, flashcard machines) reveal the deep role of path dependence. It then connects these counterfactuals to Richard Sutton's
Bitter Lesson
in artificial intelligence, suggesting that the lesson is less about the inevitability of scaling than about how scaling amplifies whichever encodings happen to lock in. Finally, it considers the Relativistic Scalar Vector Plenum (RSVP) framework as an attempt to recover "roads not taken" in the architecture of intelligence.
1. Cinematic Encodings: Frames, Scenes, and Locality
1.1 Film as 2D Frames
Modern cinema encodes experience as a sequence of 2D frames, copied identically and distributed globally. This encoding was not inevitable—it was chosen because it was cheap, reproducible, and compatible with industrial economies of scale. The consequence was the rise of the cinematic monoculture: every audience around the world saw the same actors, the same images, and the same standardized version of a film.
1.2 Volumetric Alternatives
An alternative history is plausible: film could have been encoded as
3D volumetric scenes
, capturing actors, lighting, and staging in geometric form. Each theater could then re-project the same scene from slightly different perspectives, allowing audiences to perceive variations akin to a play. Editing would resemble directing, not splicing reels. Such an encoding would have emphasized spatiality and presence over uniformity.
1.3 Local Actor Substitution
Another road not taken is the
distributed-theatrical model
. Instead of distributing a single reel worldwide, film studios might have distributed scripts, blocking instructions, and directorial cues. Each city or town would re-shoot or re-perform the film with local actors, preserving the plot and dialogue but tailoring the embodiment. This would have produced a cinema closer to theater: globally coherent but locally diverse.
2. Computational Encodings: ASCII, Cursive, and Flashcards
2.1 The ASCII-Keyboard Ratchet
Digital computation today is dominated by ASCII encoding, keyboard input, and screen output. This standard ratcheted into place through early typewriter compatibility, teletype systems, and network protocols. As with cinema's frame encoding, the choice was pragmatic and industrially efficient, but not inevitable.
2.2 Cursive and Pen-Based Machines
One alternative is the
cursive computer
: input through handwriting recognition, output via automated pens. Instead of text streams encoded as ASCII, computation could have revolved around strokes, ligatures, and calligraphic patterns. "Programming" in such a medium would resemble writing letters or marginalia, rather than typing lines of code.
2.3 Flashcard Machines
Another possibility is the
flashcard computer
: devices designed to ingest, manipulate, and output structured visual cards. Information would be stored as modular question-answer units, rather than linear streams of text. This would orient computing toward
memory augmentation
and didactic interaction, rather than textual abstraction.
3. Encoding Ratchets and the Bitter Lesson
3.1 Sutton's Claim
Richard Sutton's
Bitter Lesson
(2019) argued that progress in AI comes primarily from scaling up general-purpose methods with more compute, not from clever, domain-specific algorithms. Deep Blue and AlphaGo exemplify this brute force trajectory, and large language models appear to confirm it.
3.2 The Counter-Reading
Yet this reading overlooks contingency. Symbolic AI and expert systems of the 1970s-1990s may have "failed" not because they were wrong, but because they lacked access to GPUs, distributed training, and internet-scale data. They brought knowledge to a compute gunfight they could not win. In short: scaling amplifies—but what it amplifies depends on which encoding happened to dominate when compute became abundant.
3.3 Parallel to Cinema and Computing
Just as ASCII keyboards foreclosed cursive or flashcard computing, and 2D reels foreclosed volumetric or local-actor cinema, deep learning's synergy with GPUs has foreclosed alternative paradigms. Sutton's "bitter lesson" may therefore be a lesson not about compute per se, but about
ratchet effects
: once an encoding fits compute well enough to scale, it suppresses alternatives, whether or not those alternatives might ultimately have scaled more efficiently.
4. RSVP and Roads Not Taken in Intelligence
The RSVP (Relativistic Scalar Vector Plenum) framework can be read as an effort to re-open suppressed trajectories. Unlike token-based models that inherit the ASCII lineage, RSVP treats intelligence as a
field-theoretic process
of scalar, vector, and entropic interactions. This encoding is closer to volumetric cinema than to frame-based film: global coherence is maintained, but local instantiations (different actors, different trajectories, different semantic tilings) are possible. In this sense, RSVP offers a "local-actor cinema" for intelligence: a globally constrained but locally variable architecture of meaning.

Conclusion
The history of film and computing demonstrates how seemingly small encoding choices can ratchet into global monopolies, foreclosing alternatives that might have flourished under different conditions. Sutton's "bitter lesson" may thus be misread as a deterministic law of compute, when it is better seen as a story about how scaling amplifies contingent encodings. RSVP reimagines intelligence precisely by refusing ASCII- and token-centric ratchets, proposing instead a pluralistic, field-based path that keeps the roads not taken open.

revised and expanded version

historical case studies

Roads Not Taken: Cinema, Computation, and the Bitter Lesson
Introduction
The trajectories of cinema and computation seem inevitable in hindsight: films are linear reels of frames; computers are text-stream machines with keyboards and ASCII. Yet these were not natural laws, but
encoding ratchets
: representational choices that, once standardized, locked in global infrastructures and foreclosed alternatives. This essay explores counterfactual trajectories in cinema (volumetric scene encoding, local actor substitution) and computing (cursive and flashcard machines), situating them alongside Richard Sutton's
Bitter Lesson
in artificial intelligence. The claim is that scaling amplifies whatever encoding "wins early," not necessarily what is most efficient or generative in principle. Finally, the Relativistic Scalar Vector Plenum (RSVP) framework is introduced as an attempt to reopen those suppressed paths.
1. Cinema's Encoding Ratchets
1.1 Frame-Based Film
Film's standard encoding emerged in the 1890s: a strip of celluloid divided into sequential frames. Its advantages were simplicity, reproducibility, and compatibility with mass distribution. Once standardized, this representation defined what "film" meant: a globally identical artifact, projected identically everywhere.
1.2 Case Study: Early Stereoscopic Film
As early as 1915, filmmakers experimented with
3D stereoscopic projection
. Edwin S. Porter (of
The Great Train Robbery
) developed a dual-strip system for depth perception. But costs and technical complexity prevented it from scaling. Had it scaled, cinema might have normalized
volumetric experience
: audiences perceiving spatial depth rather than flat frames, making later VR/AR technologies feel like natural extensions.
1.3 Case Study: Localized Cinema
In the 1910s-1930s, multiple studios ran
local-language re-shoots
of films. Universal Pictures produced Spanish-language versions of its hits (
Dracula
was re-filmed with a Mexican cast in 1931). Indian and Chinese industries localized Western plots with local actors. This suggests an unrealized trajectory: cinema as a
distributed play
, where the plot is fixed but embodiment varies city by city. Had this scaled, global cinema might resemble today's theater festivals—shared narrative skeletons, locally embodied.
2. Computation's Encoding Ratchets
2.1 ASCII and the Keyboard
By the 1960s, ASCII and the QWERTY keyboard had become dominant. Their success was contingent: ASCII was chosen in 1963 as a U.S. standard largely for military and telecommunication compatibility. This choice ratcheted the entire computational ecosystem into
text-stream encoding
.
2.2 Case Study: Pen-Based Computers
The idea of handwriting recognition predates digital computers. In the 1950s, IBM prototyped the
Stenograph
and later the
IBM 1418 handwriting recognizer
, designed to read cursive input on tablets. By the early 1990s, Apple's
Newton MessagePad
revived this line. But failures in accuracy and high costs reinforced the ASCII ratchet. Had these systems matured earlier, computing could have developed as
cursive-to-pen output cycles
, treating digital machines as extended calligraphic tools.
2.3 Case Study: Flashcard and Memory Machines
Vannevar Bush's
Memex
(1945) was not a typewriter-machine but a
desk with microfilm and indexed cards
. His vision resembled a flashcard computer: modular entries, linked associations, and visual browsing. Similarly, early "teaching machines" by B. F. Skinner in the 1950s worked through flashcards and modular drills. These were eclipsed by text-stream mainframes, but they indicate a latent trajectory: computation as
memory augmentation
rather than text abstraction.
3. Encoding Ratchets and the Bitter Lesson
3.1 Sutton's Claim
Sutton's
Bitter Lesson
(2019) argued that clever, domain-specific algorithms repeatedly lose to simple methods that scale with more compute. Deep Blue, AlphaGo, and GPT confirm this trajectory.
3.2 Counter-Reading
But just as early stereoscopic and localized cinema failed for infrastructural reasons, so too did symbolic AI fail in the 1970s-1990s. It did not have access to GPUs, distributed training, or web-scale corpora. Thus, the fact that symbolic methods "lost" says less about their inherent inefficacy than about the
timing of their compute context
.
3.3 The Ratchet Effect
Scaling amplifies whatever encoding wins the early infrastructure race:
Film
: 2D frames scaled cheaply, suppressing volumetric or local variants.
Computing
: ASCII scaled cheaply, suppressing cursive and flashcard paradigms.
AI
: Neural networks scaled on GPUs, suppressing symbolic and hybrid approaches.
The Bitter Lesson, reinterpreted, is not that "scaling always wins," but that
the scalable encoding that locks in first wins by default.
4. RSVP and Reopening Suppressed Trajectories
The RSVP framework seeks to resist such ratchets. Instead of treating intelligence as token streams (the ASCII inheritance of LLMs), RSVP encodes it as
field dynamics
of scalar (
Φ
\Phi
Φ
), vector (
v
\mathbf{v}
v
), and entropy (
S
S
S
) flows. This is analogous to volumetric film: a global coherent structure that allows
local variation
(different trajectories, different instantiations).
In cinema terms: RSVP is like a
local-actor film
that preserves plot but varies embodiment.
In computation terms: RSVP is like a
flashcard/cursive hybrid
that emphasizes semantic fields and recursive memory over ASCII streams.
RSVP thus exemplifies a post-ratchet design principle: intelligence that is not bound to a single encoding, but instead
polycomputational
, allowing multiple encodings to scale together.

Conclusion
The history of film and computing shows how
encoding choices ratchet into place
. Frame-based film suppressed volumetric and localized cinema; ASCII computing suppressed cursive and flashcard machines. Sutton's
Bitter Lesson
can be reinterpreted in this light: compute scaling favors the early winners of representational ratchets, not necessarily the paradigms with deepest long-term potential. RSVP, by reopening multiple encoding paths, models a future where intelligence does not depend on a single ratchet, but on the structured interplay of many.

A novel approach to cosmological non-linearities as an effective fluid
Leonardo Giani,1, ∗ Rodrigo von Marttens,2, 3 and Ryan Camilleri1
1School of Mathematics and Physics, The University of Queensland, Brisbane, QLD 4072, Australia
2
Instituto de F´ısica, Universidade Federal da Bahia, 40210-340, Salvador, BA, Brazil
3PPGCosmo, Universidade Federal do Esp´ırito Santo, Vit´oria-ES, 29075-910, Brazil
We propose a two parameters extension of the flat ΛCDM model to capture the impact of matter
inhomogeneities on our cosmological inference. Non virialized but non-linearly evolving overdense
and underdense regions, whose abundance is quantified using the Press-Schechter formalism, are
collectively described by two effective perfect fluids ρc, ρv with non vanishing equation of state
parameters wc,v ̸= 0. These fluids are coupled to the pressureless dust, akin to an interacting DM-
DE scenario. The resulting phenomenology is very rich, and could potentially address a number
of inconsistencies of the standard model, including a simultaneous resolution of the Hubble and σ8
tensions. To assess the viability of the model, we set initial conditions compatible to the Planck 2018
best fit ΛCDM cosmology and fit its additional parameters using SN Ia observations from DESY5,
BAO distances from DESI DR2 and a sample of uncorrelated fσ8 measurements. Our findings show
that backreaction effects from the cosmic web could restore the concordance between early and late
Universe cosmological probes.
I. INTRODUCTION
Within the concordance model ΛCDM, Cosmic Mi-
crowave Background (CMB) observations [1] suggest that
the cosmic web of Large Scale Structures (LSS) we ob-
serve in the Universe emerged from small perturbations
of an otherwise homogeneous, isotropic and spatially flat
background. Huge progress has been made towards a
better understanding of the cosmic web and its evolu-
tion, in particular through numerical simulations in both
a Newtonian [2-5] and relativistic context [6-12].
However, the impact of LSS on the global evolution of
the Universe, usually referred to as cosmological backre-
action [13-24], is still a matter of debate. It is expected
that averaging on sufficiently large scales the familiar
FLRW metric is recovered, with backreaction described
by some effective modification of the Friedmann equa-
tions. Different approaches lead to different conclusions,
with a spectrum ranging from no appreciable impact [25-
28] to backreaction being responsible for the (apparent)
accelerated expansion of the Universe [29-35] (see also
Refs. [36-46] for examples of the intermediate spectrum,
and Ref. [47] for a proposed classification of backreac-
tion models). Another possibility [48] is to model the
impact of the non-linearities using the formalism of Ef-
fective Field Theory (EFT) of LSS [49, 50].
At present, there seems to be a consensus [51-54]
that backreaction from virialized structures is negligi-
ble. However, a non-negligible impact from non-virialized
structures with characteristic scale ℓ ≥ 1 Mpc in our cos-
mological inference cannot be excluded, as supported by
a number of investigations performing averages of cosmo-
logical observables in exact inhomogeneous spacetimes
[55, 56] and relativistic simulations [57-59]. These re-
sults support the idea that backreaction should vanish if
∗ l.giani@uq.edu.au
averages are performed in constant time hyphersurfaces
in the Newtonian Gauge, whose existence and connection
with real observers is still unclear.
In this letter, we propose a novel approach to model
these averages of the non-linearities and inhomogeneities
as an effective fluid, and explore its impact on cosmo-
logical observables. The resulting phenomenology has
interesting implications for a number of inconsistencies
of the ΛCDM model, including the Hubble and σ8 ten-
sions [60], and the preference for Dynamical Dark Energy
(DDE) hinted by Sn Ia and BAO measurements from
DES [61] and DESI [62, 63] when combined with CMB
observations.
II. EFFECTIVE FLUID DESCRIPTION

matter density ρtot consist of dust (ρd) and two effective
fluids (ρc and ρv) describing backreaction effects from
clustering regions and expanding voids. We couple these
effective fluids to the dust, since they emerge at late times
as a result of the gravitational interactions between dust
fluid elements. Under these hypotheses, the Friedmann
equations takes the form

2. Relation to Backreaction Literature
The classical "backreaction problem" asks: does the growth of cosmic structure feed back into the overall FLRW background dynamics?
Prior approaches:
Buchert averaging
(scalar averaging of inhomogeneous cosmologies).
Relativistic simulations
(which generally find small but not necessarily negligible effects).
Effective Field Theory of LSS
, treating nonlinearities perturbatively.
The consensus so far: virialized structures have negligible effect on cosmic expansion, but
non-virialized large-scale structures
(∼Mpc voids and filaments) might not.
This model provides a
phenomenological bridge
: instead of averaging Einstein's equations directly, one parameterizes the effect as extra fluid components with specific couplings.

Appendix A — Mapping RSVP to Effective-Fluid Backreaction (after Giani-von Marttens-Camilleri, 2025)
This appendix gives an operational correspondence between the Relativistic Scalar-Vector Plenum (RSVP) framework and effective-fluid backreaction models that represent non-linear inhomogeneities by two coupled perfect fluids

They can be integrated — and in fact, the connection is quite natural once you realize that
both essays are about how encoding choices (ratchets) shape what scales, and what then looks "natural" or "inevitable."
Let me sketch how one could merge them:
1. Shared Theme
Essay on cinema/computation
: Argues that technologies ratchet around particular encodings (ASCII vs. cursive, frames vs. volumetric film), and scaling then reinforces those encodings while suppressing alternatives.
Appendix on cosmological effective fluids
: Shows that cosmology also "chooses encodings": non-linear inhomogeneities are either averaged away, or re-encoded as effective fluids (
ρ

,
ρ
v
\rho_c,\rho_v
ρ
c
​
,
ρ
v
​
), shaping the background cosmology we infer.
Both are stories about
representation and averaging
: once a representation wins, it sets the path of research and the interpretation of data.
2. Integration Strategy
To fold them together:
Broaden the ratchet idea
— not only cultural/technological (cinema, ASCII, GPUs) but also scientific/theoretical (cosmology's choice of effective descriptions).
Frame effective fluids as an "encoding ratchet"
— Giani et al. chose to encode non-linearities as two fluids with equations of state. RSVP offers a different encoding: scalar-vector-entropy fields.
Emphasize that ratchets determine scaling
— just as ASCII scaled with Moore's Law, so too does ΛCDM + perturbation theory scale computationally better than backreaction models. RSVP is an attempt to reopen suppressed encodings.
3. Example of Merged Narrative
Just as cinema might have been encoded volumetrically or locally re-embodied, and just as computing might have grown from cursive or flashcards rather than ASCII, cosmology too has ratcheted into a particular encoding of reality. The ΛCDM model treats inhomogeneities as perturbations on a smooth background; when these non-linearities resist perturbative treatment, they are often "averaged away." Giani, von Marttens, and Camilleri show that one can instead re-encode them as effective fluids, much like choosing to re-encode a film not as fixed frames but as volumetric scenes. RSVP pushes further: it supplies a plenum-based encoding where non-linearities are not approximated by fluids but arise as intrinsic field interactions. Here again, the bitter lesson holds—compute amplifies whatever encoding we have locked into. The question is not whether scaling works, but
which encoding do we want to let scale?
4. Why They Belong Together
Both essays are about
alternative histories
: paths foreclosed not because they were wrong, but because they scaled poorly in their time.
Both show that
representational choices constrain interpretation
: ASCII vs. cursive computers, frames vs. volumetric cinema, ΛCDM perturbations vs. effective fluids vs. RSVP.
Both suggest a
meta-lesson
: scaling is not the whole story; it multiplies the consequences of whichever encoding was ratcheted in early.
So they are not only related — they fit together into a general philosophy of science and technology:
"Scaling ratchets" shape the trajectories of media, machines, and models.

so that it explicitly includes this cosmological case as a third "ratchet" (after cinema and computation), positioning RSVP as the alternative encoding to ΛCDM+effective fluids? That way it reads as a single integrated argument across media, computation, and cosmology.

Here is a rewritten version of the earlier essay, now integrated with the cosmological case. I've kept the tone consistent with your request for academic prose and made the "ratchet" theme span cinema, computation, and cosmology.
Roads Not Taken: Encoding Ratchets in Cinema, Computation, and Cosmology
Introduction
Technological and scientific histories often appear linear in retrospect. Cinema seems to have evolved naturally from silent reels to sound and digital projection; computing seems to have flowed inevitably from keyboards and ASCII to the internet and large language models; cosmology seems to progress straightforwardly from Einstein's field equations to the ΛCDM concordance model. Yet these apparent trajectories conceal a deeper truth:
each domain ratcheted around a contingent encoding choice
, and scaling thereafter amplified that choice while suppressing alternatives.
This essay examines three cases—cinema, computation, and cosmology—to argue that scaling does not reveal inevitabilities but entrenches representational accidents. The Relativistic Scalar Vector Plenum (RSVP) framework exemplifies an attempt to reopen suppressed paths, providing an alternative encoding for intelligence and for cosmological structure.
1. Cinema's Encoding Ratchet
1.1 Frames as the Chosen Representation
The encoding that dominated cinema was the
2D sequence of frames
on celluloid reels. This was chosen for pragmatic reasons: frames were easy to reproduce, ship, and project. Once industrial infrastructures aligned with this encoding, cinema became a monoculture: the same actors, the same images, distributed globally.
1.2 Roads Not Taken
Alternative encodings were available:
Volumetric scenes
: early stereoscopic experiments (Porter, 1915) hinted that cinema could have scaled as three-dimensional stage captures, enabling theaters to re-project scenes from different perspectives.
Local actor substitution
: in the 1930s, studios produced Spanish-language versions of Hollywood films, re-shooting the same scripts with local casts. Had this practice scaled, global cinema might resemble theater festivals: common plots, locally embodied.
Cinema ratcheted into frame-based encoding, foreclosing volumetric and distributed alternatives.
2. Computation's Encoding Ratchet
2.1 ASCII and the Keyboard
By the 1960s, the ASCII standard and the QWERTY keyboard had become the lingua franca of computation. This choice was contingent, driven by teletype compatibility and military communication needs. Once adopted, ASCII ratcheted computing into
linear text-stream processing
.
2.2 Roads Not Taken
Cursive computers
: IBM's handwriting recognizers (1950s) and Apple's Newton (1990s) show that computation could have scaled around cursive input and mechanical pen output, treating machines as extended calligraphic tools.
Flashcard machines
: Vannevar Bush's
Memex
(1945) and Skinner's teaching machines envisioned computation as modular flashcard-like interactions, oriented toward memory augmentation rather than text-stream abstraction.
ASCII scaled with Moore's law; cursive and flashcards were locked out.
3. Cosmology's Encoding Ratchet
3.1 ΛCDM and Perturbative Averaging
Modern cosmology ratcheted into the ΛCDM paradigm: a homogeneous FLRW background with perturbations describing structure growth. Inhomogeneities were averaged away, with non-linear regions assumed not to back-react on the global expansion. This representation fit well with perturbation theory and computational scaling, but it was not inevitable.
3.2 Roads Not Taken
Effective-fluid cosmology
: Giani, von Marttens, and Camilleri (2025) propose modeling non-virialized non-linear structures as two effective fluids,
ρ
c
\rho_c
ρ
c
​
(collapsing) and
ρ
v
\rho_v
ρ
v
​
(voids), with nonzero equations of state. This alternative encoding of backreaction suggests that tensions in ΛCDM (Hubble,
σ
8
\sigma_8
σ
8
​
) may be resolved by recognizing non-linearities as effective dark energy.
RSVP cosmology
: The Relativistic Scalar Vector Plenum framework goes further. Instead of encoding non-linearities as fluids, it represents them as intrinsic field interactions (
Φ
,
v
,
S
\Phi,\mathbf{v},S
Φ
,
v
,
S
): scalar densities, vector flows, and entropy fields. Backreaction is not an "extra" fluid but the natural outcome of field coupling and entropic transfer.
Just as cinema foreclosed volumetric representation, cosmology foreclosed non-linear encodings. ΛCDM scaled computationally better, but alternatives may yet prove more faithful.
4. The Bitter Lesson Revisited
Richard Sutton's
Bitter Lesson
claims that in AI, clever domain-specific designs lose to simple, general methods that scale with compute. But as with cinema and cosmology, the deeper truth is that
scaling amplifies whichever encoding happened to ratchet in early.
ASCII scaled better than cursive.
Frame reels scaled better than volumetric scenes.
ΛCDM scaled better than backreaction models.
This does not prove those encodings are uniquely correct—only that they fit the infrastructures available at the time.
5. RSVP as an Anti-Ratchet Architecture
RSVP embodies an explicit resistance to ratchets:
In cognition and computation, it treats intelligence as
field evolution
, not token streams.
In cosmology, it encodes inhomogeneities as
scalar-vector-entropy couplings
, not perturbations or effective fluids.
In philosophy of science, it reclaims
roads not taken
by embedding them as concurrent processes in a polycomputational field.
Where Sutton emphasizes the power of scaling, RSVP emphasizes
the choice of encoding
: scaling is multiplicative, but only the right encodings scale into genuine explanatory power.
Conclusion
Cinema, computation, and cosmology all illustrate the same meta-lesson: technologies and theories ratchet around representational choices. Once locked in, scaling amplifies those encodings and makes alternatives invisible. The danger is mistaking what scaled for what was inevitable. RSVP offers a corrective: a framework designed to keep suppressed paths alive, to treat intelligence and cosmology as polycomputational, and to resist the ratchet effect that has constrained past trajectories.

** The GIST
# Mathematical model reveals how collapsing matter and expanding voids shape universe's evolution
by [University of Queensland](http://www.uq.edu.au/)
edited by [Gaby Clark](https://sciencex.com/help/editorial-team/), reviewed by [Robert Egan](https://sciencex.com/help/editorial-team/)
** Editors' notes
[Revealing how matter affects the evolution of the universe](https://scx2.b-cdn.net/gfx/news/2025/revealing-how-matter-a.jpg)
The plot represents the new model with the x-axis and the y-axis showing the minimum sizes of collapsing and expanding regions that can impact cosmological measurements. The contours are independent datasets. Credit: Dr. Leonardo Giani
A University of Queensland researcher has developed a new mathematical model to explain the evolution of the universe which, for the first time, includes collapsing regions of matter and expanding voids.
Dr. Leonardo Giani and a team at UQ's School of Mathematics and Physics used data from the Dark Energy Spectroscopic Instrument (DESI), which has measurements of the universe to 11 billion [light years](https://phys.org/tags/light+years/). The [research](https://doi.org/10.1103/zr92-m7py) has been published in *Physical Review Letters*.
"This new model can change the way physicists and cosmologists look at the universe," Dr. Giani said.
"The standard model mapping the universe from the Big Bang until today has matter particles of the same size not interacting with each other.
"But in reality, we can see there are stars, [black holes](https://phys.org/tags/black+holes/), [galaxy clusters](https://phys.org/tags/galaxy+clusters/) and empty regions constantly interacting via forces such as gravity not accounted for by the standard model.
"For 30 years, scientists have tried to explain what is happening as this complex universe expands and there have been plenty of exotic attempts at solutions.
"We know voids and collapsing regions exist, but we didn't really know how to compute their impact on the measurements.
"My model gives a recipe to compute it, without the need for new physics.
"This framework uses simple mathematical terms to describe how the emergence of complex structures in the universe affects our cosmological measurements."
Dr. Giani's team set out to identify the minimum size a void needs to have in order to impact the measurements (called R_v) as well as the minimum size a cluster must have (called R_c).
Independent datasets, including DESI data, were plotted against an x-axis and y-axis showing the minimum sizes of the collapsing and expanding regions that can impact cosmological measurements.
"The [standard model](https://phys.org/tags/standard+model/) of the universe should see all of these dataset contours overlap in the upper right corner, where the sizes of the expanding and contracting regions would be too large for them to exist," Dr. Giani said.
"They instead overlap in a different region, which indicates that large voids of space might be responsible for anomalous behavior observed in the data."
Dr. Giani said the new model also addresses the two biggest problems in modern cosmology—Hubble tension and the evidence for dynamical dark energy.
Hubble tension is a discrepancy between two methods of calculating the universe's expansion rate, while dynamical dark energy is a theory that says energy is not constant but changing or weakening over time.
"Our model has the richness to address both of these," Dr. Giani said.
"If you assume that dark energy is weakening and then you try to infer how fast the universe is expanding from that data, you get even a lower measurement of the expansion rate of the universe—solutions to one problem create another.
"In our model, any weakening of energy is just a detailed accounting of what the universe looks like today, it behaves as if it was weakening but may not be weakening.
"In our plot, the green box is a region where the Hubble tension is resolved.
"Essentially, when we asked if the complexity of the universe was showing up in the DESI data, the result was that it was, and our framework can explain all of the observations."

1. What are "collapsing regions" and "expanding voids"?

Roads Not Taken: Encoding Ratchets in Cinema, Computation, and Cosmology
Introduction
Histories of media, machines, and models often appear linear: cinema progresses from reels to digital projection, computation from teletypes to neural networks, cosmology from Einstein to ΛCDM. Yet these trajectories reflect not inevitability but
encoding ratchets
—arbitrary representational choices that, once standardized, scale efficiently and foreclose alternatives.
Scaling, as Richard Sutton's
Bitter Lesson
(2019) reminds us, is powerful. But scaling multiplies whichever encodings happened to lock in early, not necessarily those best suited to capture reality. This essay explores three cases—cinema, computation, and cosmology—to argue that scaling can conceal deeper alternatives. Recent work by Giani, von Marttens, and Camilleri (2025) on effective-fluid cosmology highlights how close mainstream physics is now coming to the entropic and structural intuitions formalized in the Relativistic Scalar Vector Plenum (RSVP).
1. Cinema's Encoding Ratchet
1.1 The Frame Standard
Cinema ratcheted into 2D frames on reels, chosen for ease of duplication and projection. This encoding made cinema a global monoculture: one film, one set of actors, identical everywhere.
1.2 Suppressed Alternatives
Volumetric cinema
: early stereoscopic systems suggested films could have been encoded as three-dimensional scenes, allowing each theater to re-project slightly different perspectives.
Local actor substitution
: in the 1930s, parallel-language versions of films were re-shot with local casts. Had this scaled, cinema might resemble distributed theater: global plots, local embodiments.
Frames scaled best, so they won.
2. Computation's Encoding Ratchet
2.1 ASCII and the Keyboard
Computing ratcheted into ASCII and keyboards, initially for military and telecommunication compatibility. This encoded machines as
linear text processors
, a lineage inherited today by tokenized AI models.
2.2 Suppressed Alternatives
Cursive machines
: IBM handwriting recognizers (1950s) and Apple's Newton (1990s) show computing could have scaled around cursive input and pen output.
Flashcard machines
: Vannevar Bush's
Memex
and B. F. Skinner's teaching devices envisioned modular, card-based information storage and recall—computation as memory augmentation, not stream manipulation.
ASCII scaled better, so it won.
3. Cosmology's Encoding Ratchet
3.1 The ΛCDM Paradigm
Modern cosmology ratcheted into the ΛCDM model: a homogeneous background with perturbations. Non-linear structures were treated as negligible, their backreaction assumed to vanish. This choice fit perturbation theory and computational scaling.
3.2 Suppressed Alternatives
Backreaction models
: Buchert averaging and relativistic simulations showed hints that collapsing clusters and expanding voids might matter for the global expansion, but these approaches never scaled computationally.
Effective fluids
: Giani, von Marttens, and Camilleri (2025) recently modeled collapsing and expanding regions as two effective fluids with non-zero equations of state. This representation, fit to DESI and supernova data, eases the Hubble and
σ
8
\sigma_8
σ
8
​
tensions—longstanding problems of ΛCDM.
RSVP cosmology
: RSVP represents collapse and voids not as phenomenological fluids but as intrinsic scalar-vector-entropy field dynamics. What the effective-fluid model encodes phenomenologically, RSVP derives as microphysics: compressive modes (
∇
⁣
⋅
⁣
v
<
0
\nabla\!\cdot\!\mathbf{v}<0
∇
⋅
v
<
0
), expansive modes (
∇
⁣
⋅
⁣
v
>
0
\nabla\!\cdot\!\mathbf{v}>0
∇
⋅
v
>
0
), and their couplings (lamphron-lamphrodyne transfers).
3.3 A Convergence of Intuitions
The surprise is that mainstream cosmology is now arriving, via effective fluids, at insights RSVP predicted:
The
apparent weakening of dark energy
can be reinterpreted as the entropic footprint of structure formation.
The
resolution of the Hubble tension
emerges not from new fields but from recognizing collapsing and expanding regions as active components.
The
σ8 tension
is likewise eased once effective collapse and void terms modify growth.
Here cosmology mirrors cinema and computation: a contingent encoding (smooth FLRW + perturbations) scaled best, but alternative encodings (backreaction fluids, RSVP fields) may prove richer.
4. Scaling and the Bitter Lesson
The Bitter Lesson tells us that general methods plus scaling outperform clever designs. Yet history shows scaling amplifies
whatever encoding was already ratcheted in
:
ASCII scaled better than cursive.
Frames scaled better than volumetric cinema.
ΛCDM scaled better than backreaction models.
This does not mean those encodings are uniquely right—only that they were the most scalable at the time.
5. RSVP as a Counter-Ratchet
RSVP resists ratchets by keeping multiple encodings alive:
In cognition, it encodes processes as scalar-vector-entropy field flows, not tokens.
In cosmology, it encodes backreaction as entropic dynamics, not as dark energy or effective fluids.
In philosophy of science, it suggests that suppressed encodings (local actors, flashcards, collapsing voids) can coexist as concurrent computational modes.
Where Sutton emphasizes the inevitability of scaling, RSVP emphasizes the
choice of representation
: scaling multiplies, but the quality of what it multiplies depends on whether we locked in the right encoding.
Conclusion
Cinema, computation, and cosmology all illustrate the same lesson:
what scaled is not necessarily what was most true, only what was most compatible with infrastructure at the time.
Recent effective-fluid models of collapse and voids show that even cosmology is rediscovering the power of alternative encodings. RSVP goes further, embedding those encodings in a unified field-theoretic plenum.
The roads not taken in cinema and computation produced monocultures; the road not taken in cosmology may have obscured the real drivers of the Universe's apparent acceleration. RSVP stands as a reminder that suppressed paths can be reopened—and that the future of intelligence and cosmology alike depends not only on scaling, but on choosing the right encodings before we let scaling run away.

Sidebar 1 (Backreaction in plain terms):
Imagine stirring a pot of thick soup. Some regions clump into denser lumps; other regions thin out into bubbles. If you treat the soup as perfectly uniform, you will mis-estimate how it heats and circulates. "Backreaction" is simply the recognition that lumps and voids feed back into the global behavior of the whole pot.

Note 1 (Dark energy "weakening"):
In ΛCDM, dark energy is assumed constant. But if you ignore the effect of voids and clusters, the data
looks
like dark energy is changing. Giani's model shows that what appears to be "weakening energy" is really just the uneven texture of the universe being misread. RSVP makes a similar claim: the cosmos does not need literal expansion—apparent acceleration is the echo of entropic redistribution.
Note 2 (Hubble tension in plain terms):
The Hubble tension is the mismatch between two rulers: the early-universe ruler (CMB) and the late-universe ruler (supernovae, BAO). If you account for voids and collapsing regions, you are not stretching the ruler, just admitting that it is dented and bent in places. The mismatch shrinks.
4. Scaling and the Bitter Lesson
The Bitter Lesson tells us that general methods plus scaling outperform clever designs. Yet history shows scaling amplifies
whatever encoding was already ratcheted in
:
ASCII scaled better than cursive.
Frames scaled better than volumetric cinema.
ΛCDM scaled better than backreaction models.
This does not mean those encodings are uniquely right—only that they were the most scalable at the time.

RSVP resists ratchets by keeping multiple encodings alive:
In cognition, it encodes processes as scalar-vector-entropy field flows, not tokens.
In cosmology, it encodes backreaction as entropic dynamics, not as dark energy or effective fluids.
In philosophy of science, it suggests that suppressed encodings (local actors, flashcards, collapsing voids) can coexist as concurrent computational modes.

choice of representation

what scaled is not necessarily what was most true, only what was most compatible with infrastructure at the time.

A.2 Ratchets in Computational Architecture
The essay emphasized ASCII and keyboards as ratchets of
representation
. But an equally consequential ratchet occurred in
computer architecture
: the adoption of the
von Neumann model
in the 1940s. This architecture—single memory for code and data, sequential instruction execution—scaled brilliantly with early hardware, but it was not inevitable.
A.2.1 The von Neumann Ratchet
Advantages
: Simplicity of design, compatibility with symbolic programming, easy scaling of transistor counts.
Consequences
: Bottlenecks in memory bandwidth ("von Neumann bottleneck"), strong orientation toward sequential symbolic processing, and reinforcement of the ASCII/text-stream paradigm.
A.2.2 Suppressed Alternatives
Dataflow architectures
Programs represented as graphs of data dependencies.
Computation proceeds when inputs are ready, inherently parallel.
Early experiments (MIT Tagged Token architecture, 1970s) showed promise but could not scale cheaply with hardware of the time.
Ratcheted out by the dominance of sequential instruction sets.
Neural and analog computing
Mechanical/electronic analog machines in the 1930s-1950s solved differential equations directly.
Neuromorphic concepts (McCulloch-Pitts networks, later Carver Mead's silicon neurons) offered parallelism and efficiency.
Lost momentum in the transistor era when digital sequential logic scaled faster.
Associative / content-addressable memory
Instead of fetching data by address, retrieval is pattern-based ("give me the row that matches this key").
Would have supported memory-intensive AI tasks naturally.
Remained niche (in networking hardware, caches) while mainstream computing entrenched address-based access.
Stack and functional machines
Burroughs B5000 (1961) embodied a stack machine with direct support for high-level languages.
Functional programming languages (LISP, FP) could have ratcheted differently if stack machines had scaled.
Instead, C and von Neumann-style imperative languages dominated, reinforcing text-stream ASCII encodings.
A.2.3 The RSVP Counterfactual
From the RSVP perspective, these suppressed architectures resemble
roads not taken in cosmology
:
von Neumann = ΛCDM perturbation theory (efficient scaling, but rigid encoding).
Dataflow = backreaction/field encoding (explicit non-linearity, natural parallelism).
Analog/neuromorphic = RSVP plenum simulation itself (continuous fields, entropic flows).
Associative memory = TARTAN/RSVP semantic tilings (pattern-based access, trajectory memory).
Had dataflow or neuromorphic systems scaled first, the culture of computation might have favored RSVP-style field encodings from the beginning rather than ASCII streams.
A.3 Ratchets and Polycomputational Futures
The lesson from both cosmology and computation is that
ratchets lock in encodings
that scale best with available hardware, even if they are not the most expressive.
In cosmology, ΛCDM suppressed non-linear encodings; effective-fluid models and RSVP reopen them.
In computation, von Neumann suppressed dataflow, associative, and analog architectures; RSVP-inspired systems could revive them for a polycomputational future.
The danger, as Sutton's Bitter Lesson shows, is mistaking what scaled for what was inevitable. The task now is to resist single ratchets by designing architectures—scientific and computational—that
preserve multiplicity of encodings
. RSVP is one such attempt: a plenum in which collapse and expansion, token and field, discrete and continuous, can all scale together.

Thus, what the effective-fluid model represents phenomenologically, RSVP produces dynamically. The fact that mainstream cosmology has rediscovered these terms is evidence that RSVP's encoding captures genuine physical structure.
A.2 Ratchets in Computational Architecture
The adoption of the
von Neumann architecture
—a sequential model where a single memory stores both instructions and data—was not inevitable. It scaled well with vacuum tubes and later transistors, but other architectures existed that might have locked in had conditions been slightly different.
A.2.1 Dataflow Machines
Concept
: Programs represented as dependency graphs. Computation proceeds when inputs are ready; instructions fire asynchronously.
Examples
: MIT Tagged Token Dataflow Machine (1970s), Manchester Dataflow Computer (1980s).
Promise
: Natural parallelism; eliminates the von Neumann bottleneck.
Failure to scale
: Hardware complexity and lack of compiler support made them slower and more expensive than von Neumann machines in the short term.
Counterfactual
: If dataflow had scaled, programming would look more like wiring RSVP's field equations directly—flows, dependencies, and event-driven computation—rather than issuing sequential instructions.
A.2.2 Analog and Neuromorphic Computing
Analog machines
: Vannevar Bush's Differential Analyzer (1930s) solved differential equations with gears, shafts, and wheels.
Neuromorphic chips
: Carver Mead (1980s) pioneered silicon neurons and synapses, aiming for brainlike computation.
Promise
: Direct representation of continuous dynamics; energy-efficient and massively parallel.
Failure to scale
: Digital logic was easier to miniaturize and manufacture reliably.
Counterfactual
: An analog/neuromorphic ratchet would have normalized RSVP-style continuous field dynamics, making scalar-vector-entropy simulation the
default
mode of computing.
A.2.3 Associative and Content-Addressable Memory (CAM)
Concept
: Retrieve data by its content, not by an address. Ask the memory, "Who matches this pattern?" rather than "What is stored at address X?"
Examples
: Early associative memories in the 1960s; used in networking and caches but never mainstream.
Promise
: Pattern recognition and search become fundamental operations—ideal for AI.
Failure to scale
: Too costly in silicon area; industry standardized on address-based RAM.
Counterfactual
: An associative-memory ratchet would have fit perfectly with RSVP's TARTAN framework: computation as semantic tiling and trajectory memory, not instruction-address cycles.
A.2.4 Stack and Functional Machines
Burroughs B5000 (1961)
: A pioneering stack machine designed to run ALGOL directly. Programs executed as high-level blocks; hardware understood recursion.
Promise
: Close alignment between human reasoning (functions, recursion) and machine execution.
Failure to scale
: Less efficient for low-level tasks, overtaken by IBM System/360 and C-based architectures.
Counterfactual
: If the B5000 lineage had won, programming culture might have ratcheted toward functional abstraction and symbolic manipulation, not imperative token streams. RSVP's categorical formulations would feel less alien in such an ecosystem.
A.3 RSVP and Architectural Counterfactuals
Seen through RSVP, each alternative architecture corresponds to a "road not taken" in computation, much like effective-fluid cosmology represents a road not taken in physics:
Von Neumann ↔ ΛCDM perturbation theory
: Scales well, but encodes reality narrowly.
Dataflow ↔ backreaction averaging
: Emphasizes flows and dependencies, natural parallelism, but too costly early on.
Analog/neuromorphic ↔ RSVP plenum
: Continuous fields, entropic flows, microphysical realism; suppressed by digital ratchet.
Associative memory ↔ TARTAN/semantic tilings
: Pattern-based retrieval and modular computation; eclipsed by address-based memory.
Stack machines ↔ categorical/sheaf encodings
: Function-first design, structurally aligned with higher mathematics; suppressed by imperative hardware/software co-evolution.
A.4 Polycomputational Futures
The common lesson across cinema, computation, and cosmology is that
ratchets lock in whichever encoding scaled first
, suppressing equally valid alternatives. RSVP is explicitly designed to resist such ratchets by enabling
polycomputation
: multiple encodings (field, token, associative, functional) scaling together, rather than one monopolizing all resources.
In cosmology, this means entropic backreaction alongside ΛCDM-like averages.
In computation, this means associative, neuromorphic, and dataflow components running in concert with sequential processors.
In culture, this means volumetric cinema alongside frame reels, or local actor substitutions alongside global blockbusters.
Scaling is not the enemy; it is the multiplier. But only if we keep the encodings plural.
